L1712: A slight, but not statistically significant delay in sexual maturation in males (balano-preputial separation) and females (vaginal patency) at \ge 30 mg/kg that was within the laboratory historical control data range. Delays in sexual maturation have been observed with other SGLT2 inhibitors. While the delay in males could possibly be attributed to a decrease in body weight between PND 31 and PND 35 (7-8%, relative to controls), there were no treatment-related reductions in body weight in females. Given that the delay was slight and within the laboratory historical control data range, it is not a significant clinical concern.
L1713: Similar to the adults, there was minimal metabolism of sotagliflozin to sotagliflozin-3-Oglucuronide (main human metabolite) in juvenile rats.
L1714: ## 10 Special Toxicology Studies
L1715: ## 10.1 Phototoxicity
L1716: There were no phototoxicity studies conducted given that sotagliflozin does not absorb within the 300 to 800 nm range of the electronic spectrum.
L1717: ## 10.2 Immunogenicity
L1718: Specific immunotoxicity studies were not considered necessary. No treatment-related effects on myelosuppression, changes in the immune system organ weights or histology, or hematological changes in the repeat dose studies in the rat and dog are indicative of immunotoxicity.
L1719: ## 10.3 Abuse Potential
L1720: A weight-of-evidence assessment based on available literature data related to SGLT inhibition, animal toxicology data, and clinical data concluded that sotagliflozin's potential risk for abuse is very low.
L1721: ## 10.4 Local Tolerance
L1722: The irritant or corrosive potential of sotagliflozin was evaluated in male New Zealand White rabbits. Sotagliflozin (0.5 g) applied under occlusion for 4 hours to an area of skin did not cause erythema or edema up to 72 hours after patch removal indicating that sotagliflozin was not a dermal irritant.
L1723: ## 10.5 LX4211-n107 In Silico Analysis of Impurities of Sotagliflozin
L1724: A mutagenic risk assessment was performed on actual and potential mutagenic impurities of sotagliflozin. Potential and actual impurities were investigated for structural alerts for mutagenicity using two quantitative structure-activity relationship (QSAR) in silico systems. Expert rule-based methodology and parameters were assessed using Leadscope Genetox Expert Alerts v7 (System: Leadscope Model Applier v3.0.2.4). Statistical-based methodology and parameters were assessed using Leadscope Bacterial Mutation Statistical-Based QSAR model v2.
L1725: A total of 50 impurities were assessed. No impurities from the cohorts of concern, Class 1 (i.e., known mutagenic carcinogens), Class 2 (i.e., known mutagen), or Class 4 (i.e., bearing structure alert for mutagenicity, also present in drug substance or compounds related to the drug substance that are known to be non-mutagenic), were identified.
L1726: When impurities do not have accompanying carcinogenicity or bacterial mutagenicity data and undergo a Structure-Activity Relationship assessment, a class 3 designation is given when an alerting structure(s) is identified or there are no mutagenicity data. Three impurities were considered as Class 3:
L1727: (b) (4)
L1728: The acceptable intake for each of these 3 impurities was set as (b) (4) \mu\text{g}/\text{day}, as described in CMC section (Module 3 Section S.3.2 – Impurities, subsection 6 Mutagenic Impurities).
L1729: The remaining 47 impurities were classified as ICH Class 5 (non-mutagenic) based on the QSAR assessment or the availability of data or information to refute a positive QSAR finding.
L1730: ## 11 Integrated Summary and Safety Evaluation
L1731: Sotagliflozin (LX4211) is an orally administered small molecule, dual sodium-glucose cotransporter 1 (SGLT1) and sodium-glucose cotransporter 2 (SGLT2) inhibitor. Sotagliflozin is being developed for adults with heart failure (b) (4) acute or worsening heart failure; and adults with type 2 diabetes mellitus, chronic kidney disease, and other cardiovascular risk factors, including a history of heart
L1732: failure. The recommended regimen starts with 200 mg sotagliflozin tablet once daily (QD) before the first meal of the day and is increased to 400 mg sotagliflozin tablet QD in patients tolerating the 200 mg dosage.
L1733: Sotagliflozin is a potent dual inhibitor of human SGLT2 (IC_{50} 1.8 nM) and SGLT1 (IC_{50} 36.3 nM). SGLT2 inhibition blocks glucose reabsorption in the renal proximal tubules resulting in increased urinary glucose excretion (UGE). SGLT1 inhibition in the intestines may improve glucose control by reducing or delaying postprandial glucose absorption delivering more glucose distally and increasing the release of glucagon-like peptide-1 (GLP-1) and peptide YY (PYY) levels into the portal circulation, which in turn increase insulin secretion. Oral administration of sotagliflozin caused consistently significant urinary glucose excretion in mice, rats, dogs, and monkeys in a dose-dependent fashion, with ED_{50} values of ~1.8, 0.75, and 0.2 mg/kg respectively. In various animal models of diabetes mellitus (both T2DM and T1DM) or obesity, sotagliflozin inhibited SGLT1 and SGLT2, resulting in improved glycemic control and lower HbA1c levels. Chronic treatment with sotagliflozin in a ZSF-1 rat model of systemic arterial hypertension with diabetes mellitus and obesity [a metabolic rat model of heart failure with preserved ejection fraction (HFpEF)] improved *in vivo* surrogate markers of diastolic dysfunction including left ventricular end-diastolic pressure, left atrial remodeling, and isovolumetric relaxation time, resulted in recovery of heart rate and increased cardiac output (Study SED00079).
L1734: Sotagliflozin also reduces sodium reabsorption in the proximal tubules and increases the delivery of sodium to the distal tubules of the kidney1, 2, 3. This may influence several physiological functions including, but not restricted to, lowering both pre- and afterload of the heart and downregulation of sympathetic activity, and decreased intraglomerular pressure.
L1735: Sotagliflozin may improve metabolic adaptation of the cardiomyocyte to the *in vivo* conditions related to cardiac adverse remodeling in diabetic cardiomyopathy. All these effects may be beneficial to adults with type 2 diabetes mellitus regarding cardiovascular (CV) and renal outcomes.
L1736: Safety pharmacology studies assessing the cardiovascular, neurological, respiratory, renal, and gastrointestinal effects of sotagliflozin did not identify any acute safety concerns at clinical exposure levels (NOAELs 100 mg/kg in dogs or rats, \ge 24X MHRD13).
L1737: The pharmacokinetics (PK) of sotagliflozin has been assessed in mice, rats, dogs, and monkeys after both intravenous (IV) and oral dosing. Sotagliflozin is rapidly absorbed across species after oral dosing with a bioavailability in rats and dogs (50-71%) that is comparable to that in the human (63%). Sotagliflozin is extensively distributed throughout the body in the rat, although levels in the CNS (brain and spinal cord), eye, bone, and bone marrow are relatively low. Plasma protein binding of sotagliflozin is high
L1738: 13 Safety margin calculation based Day 1 AUC_{0-24} value (46254 ng*h/mL) from the 4-week toxicology study in male rats dosed at 100 mg/kg.
L1739: across species including humans (&gt;91%).
L1740: Sotagliflozin is extensively metabolized in the mouse, rat, monkey, and human, but shows little apparent metabolism in the dog. Although no unique human metabolites were identified, there were significant metabolite differences between species. In humans, direct glucuronidation is the predominant route of metabolism; while in the rat and mouse, there is more oxidative metabolism in addition to glucuronidation. The sotagliflozin glucuronide conjugate, sotagliflozin-3-O-glucuronide, accounts for 94% of total radioactivity in human plasma, whereas sotagliflozin-3-O-glucuronide exposure in the rat and mouse was less than 1X relative to exposure in humans. As sotagliflozin-3-O-glucuronide has minimal pharmacological activity at SGLT1 and SGLT2 and is not an acyl glucuronide of sotagliflozin, there is no toxicological concern at clinical exposure. In vitro phenotyping indicates UGT1A9 and, to a lesser extent, CYP3A4 as enzymes responsible for the metabolism of sotagliflozin in humans.
L1741: In rats, excretion of an orally administered dose was primarily recovered in the feces (82%) with 13% excreted in the urine. Whereas, in humans, the main route of elimination was through the urine (57%) with 37% excreted in the feces.
L1742: The toxicity profile of sotagliflozin was evaluated in a single-dose rat study, in a battery of definitive repeat-dose general toxicity studies in rats (up to 26 weeks) and dogs (up to 39 weeks), in genetic toxicity studies, in mouse and rat carcinogenicity studies, and in a battery of exploratory and definitive reproductive and developmental toxicity studies in rats and rabbits. Other toxicity studies were performed as needed.
L1743: In general toxicity studies, the rat was the more sensitive one of the two species used. Target organs include the kidney (cortical tubule dilation and inflammation/hyperplasia), bladder (inflammation/hyperplasia), prostate (inflammation), bone (increased trabecular bone), and stomach (nonglandular hyperplasia/hyperkeratosis and ulcers), most of which were identifiable after 4 weeks of dosing in the rat. Adverse effects in the dog were generally limited to gastrointestinal-related clinical signs and an increase in heart rate. Thyroid (follicular cell hyperplasia/adenoma/carcinoma) was an additional target organ identified in the 2-year carcinogenicity study in rats.
L1744: Renal tubule dilation was observed at doses \ge 30 mg/kg/day in the 6-month rat study (males, AUC_{0-24} 15077 ng·h/ml, 8X MHRD) and at doses \ge 10 mg/kg/day in the 2-year study in males (AUC_{0-24} 5440 ng·h/ml, 3X MHRD) and females (AUC_{0-24} 9540 ng·h/ml, 5X MHRD). The reversible tubule dilation, which was associated with an increase in kidney weight but no biomarker changes indicating renal injury, is considered an adaptive change to the polyuria and is consistent with the findings of other SGLT2 inhibitors. Urinary tract inflammatory changes and hyperplasia/hypertrophy affecting the renal pelvis, urinary bladder, and/or urethra in the 6-month study were largely limited to the high dose females (300 mg/kg/day, AUC_{0-24} 267278 ng·h/ml, 138X MHRD); however, in the 2-year rat study, inflammatory changes and infection affecting the kidney and bladder occurred across all dose groups (\ge 10 mg/kg/day) in males (AUC_{0-24} 5440 ng·h/ml, 3X MHRD). These dose-related increases in urinary tract
L1745: inflammation/infection, which were considered secondary to pharmacodynamically mediated glucosuria and/or calculi formation, were not associated with any neoplastic changes in the urinary tract in the 2-year study at exposures up to 15X MHRD in males (AUC_{0-24} 28500 ng·h/ml) and 45X MHRD in females (AUC_{0-24} 87800 ng·h/ml). Renal changes in dogs were limited to reversible increases in kidney weight.
L1746: The no-effect dose of sotagliflozin for prostate inflammation was 30 mg/kg/day in the 6-month rat study and falls below the lowest doses evaluated in the 2-year rat studies (&lt;10mg/kg/day). Prostate inflammation was observed in all pivotal toxicology studies in rats including the juvenile animal study. Moderate to marked prostate inflammation was observed at the mid and high doses (17-54X MHRD) in the 6-month rat study with no apparent reversibility after the 4-week recovery. In the 2-year rat study, sotagliflozin caused moderate to severe prostate inflammation across all dose groups (\ge 10 mg/kg/day, 3X MHRD). The mechanism of prostate inflammation is unknown. While some cases appear to be related to urinary tract inflammation/infection, there was a general absence of bladder or other urinary tract inflammation/infection associated with the prostate inflammation in the 6-month study. The possible involvement of SGLT1, which is expressed in the prostate, cannot be excluded. No inflammation was observed in the mouse or the dog.
L1747: In the 6-month rat study, there was a dose-related increase in trabecular bone of the sternum (minimal to moderate) and decrease in the calcitropic hormones 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D and parathyroid hormone (PTH) in all dose groups (\ge 30 mg/kg/day, 8X MHRD) that are likely due to changes in calcium homeostasis as a result of intestinal SGLT1 inhibition. Only reduction in 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D levels occurred in the 9-month dog study at the mid and high dose. GI-related clinical signs including vomiting, watery feces, and diarrhea are common in dogs, which may minimize the intestinal SGLT1 inhibition-induced changes in calcium homeostasis, lead to less clinical manifestations in bones.
L1748: Higher incidences of glandular stomach acute erosions/ulcers, non-glandular stomach hyperplasia/hyperkeratosis were observed in the 4-week, 26-week, and 2-year rat studies at doses \ge 30 mg/kg/day (8X MHRD). These findings may be related to SGLT1 inhibition. However, there were no similar findings in dogs dosed up to 9 months or clinical reports of gastrointestinal injury.
L1749: There were higher incidences of thyroid follicular cell hyperplasia, adenoma, and carcinoma in all sotagliflozin dose levels in both males and females in the 2-year rat study. The thyroid follicular cell hyperplasia, adenoma, and carcinoma are continuous histological changes of pathological cellular growth. However, Independent FDA statistical analysis did not find the thyroid follicular cell carcinoma at the high dose to be statistically significant in pairwise analysis, and the ECAC concurred that there were no treatment-related increases in neoplasms in rats at doses up to 75 mg/kg [18-54X the maximal human recommended dose (MHRD)]. Without the end point of neoplasm, the significance of continuous pathological cellular growth in thyroid in rats is limited.
L1750: Reproductive and developmental toxicity were assessed in fertility and early embryonic development, embryofetal development, and pre- and post-natal development animal studies. Sotagliflozin had no effects on reproductive performance or fertility indices in male and female rats at exposures up to 55-138X the MHRD despite mortality/morbidity in females and significant reductions in weight gain in males at 19-25X the MHRD. Sotagliflozin was not teratogenic in the rat at 100 mg/kg (40X MHRD) or in the rabbit at 200 mg/kg (9X MHRD). In rats, a higher dose (350 mg/kg; 161X MHRD) caused both maternal and fetal toxicity including significant reduction in maternal weight gain, embryo-lethality, reduced fetal weights, altered sex ratio, and cardiovascular and skeletal malformations. The 200 mg/kg dose in rabbits caused significant reductions in maternal weight gain, but had no effect on fetal survival or growth, or caused any malformations.
L1751: In the post-natal development study in the rat, sotagliflozin had no effect on developmental landmarks, sexual maturation, neural behavior development, or reproductive performance of the F1 generation (up to 19-25X MRHD). However, sotagliflozin caused dilation of the renal pelvis in F1 pups exposed at \ge 30 mg/kg (4-7X MHRD) in utero and during lactation, resulting in a NOAEL of 10 mg/kg/day (1.3-2.5X MHRD for the F1 generation).
L1752: The kidney was also identified as a target organ in male and female juvenile rats with renal tubular and pelvis dilatation at 5-11X MHRD in the juvenile animal study. Increases in kidney weights and renal mineralization (males only) across all dose groups (\sim 1X MHRD) were observed as well. All treatment-related renal changes demonstrated full or partial reversibility following the 4-week recovery period.
L1753: The renal changes in rats in the post-natal development and juvenile animal studies are considered secondary to the pharmacodynamic activity of the drug and are consistent with the effects of other SGLT2 inhibitors. Given that sotagliflozin is distributed to most fetal tissues including the kidney when administered to dams during gestation and the differences in the timing of kidney development/maturation between rats and humans, the treatment-related renal effects in the juvenile rats are considered relevant to the assessment of reproductive and developmental risk. The morphological and functional renal development in the juvenile rat corresponds to renal development in humans during the late second and third trimester through approximately 2 years of age. Lactational exposure may also pose a risk to the developing human kidney as sotagliflozin was excreted in maternal milk (1.3-fold higher than plasma; AUC basis) in rats.
L1754: Sotagliflozin was not mutagenic or clastogenic in a standard battery of two in vitro and one in vivo GLP genetic toxicology studies.
L1755: The potential of sotagliflozin to induce tumors was assessed in the 6-month transgenic RasH2 mouse study and the 2-year rat carcinogenicity study. There were no statistically significant increases in neoplasms considered treatment related in transgenic RasH2 mice dosed up to 100 mg/kg. Nor were there any treatment-related neoplasms in male
L1756: (up to 15X MHRD) and female (up to 45X MHRD) rats. This negative carcinogenic outcome for sotagliflozin differs from other SGLT2 inhibitors which are typified by neoplasms of the adrenals, testes, and renal tubules. The absence of renal and adrenal tumors is particularly noteworthy for this SGLT1/2 inhibitor because the tumorigenic MOA for these tumors is reportedly carbohydrate malabsorption secondary to intestinal SGLT1 inhibition. The absence of these tumors with sotagliflozin is likely due to less carbohydrate malabsorption than observed with other class members at the doses evaluated in these studies.
L1757: In conclusion, potential safety issues identified from the nonclinical program were derived from intended pharmacological action of sotagliflozin on SGLT1 and SGLT2, rather than from compound-specific toxicity.

L1763: Figure 2 displays two line graphs showing the Hazard Ratio (HR) for the Primary Composite Outcome versus Time (Months) for the SOLOIST (left) and SCORED (right) trials. The Y-axis represents the Hazard Ratio, and the X-axis represents Time (Months) from 0 to 24 (SOLOIST) or 24 to 30 (SCORED).
In both trials, the Hazard Ratio (solid blue line) is consistently below 1.0, indicating a lower risk of the primary composite outcome in the sotagliflozin group compared to the placebo group. The 95% Confidence Interval (dashed lines) remains entirely below 1.0 throughout the follow-up period, confirming statistical significance.
SOLOIST (Left Graph): The HR starts around 0.85 at 2 months and gradually decreases to approximately 0.75 by 24 months. The 95% CI ranges from approximately 0.65 to 0.95.
SCORED (Right Graph): The HR starts around 0.95 at 2 months, dips slightly below 0.9, and then gradually decreases to approximately 0.75 by 24 months. The 95% CI ranges from approximately 0.65 to 0.95.

L1773: Diagram illustrating the SOLOIST Trial Design. The trial starts with Heart failurea and Admission for WHF (Index Event)b, leading to Randomization (Rc). The trial is divided into a Screening period, a Randomized double-blind treatment period, and a Follow-up period.
During the Randomized double-blind treatment period, patients are assigned to one of two arms:
Arm 1 (n=2000): Until Visit 4: Sotagliflozin 200 mg. Up-titration to 400 mg sotagliflozin or matching placebo (2 tablets), if tolerated: Visit 4 (or Visit 5, 6, or 7)d.
Arm 2 (n=2000): Matching placebo.
Endpoint collection (all patients) occurs during the Randomized double-blind treatment period.
Visits (V1 through V7, and FUf) are scheduled throughout the treatment period. V1 is Screening. V2 is Day 1. V3 is Wk 1. V4 is Wk 2. V5 is Wk 4. V6 is Month 4. V7 is Month 8. Visits V3 through V7 occur every 4 months. The Study Closeout Visit (FU) occurs 14 days after V7.
L1774: Diagram illustrating the SCORED Trial Design timeline.
The trial starts with a Screening period (1 to 4 weeks) leading to Randomization (R).
Randomization splits the population into two arms:
Arm 1 (N=6,290): Sotagliflozin 200 mg.
Arm 2 (N=6,290): Matching placebo.
Both arms proceed to the Randomized double-blind treatment period, followed by a Follow-up (FU) Visit (14 days).
During the treatment period, patients undergo visits (V1, V2, V3, V4, V5) at Weeks -4, 0, 4, 8, and 26, respectively. V5 is associated with on-site visits every 26 weeks until Study Closeout, with two telephone visits held in the period between consecutive on-site visits.
Up-titration to 400 mg sotagliflozin or matching placebo (2 tablets), if toleratedb, occurs after V3 (Week 4).
Endpoint collection (all patients) spans the duration of the Randomized double-blind treatment period.

L1782: Figure 4.3.1.1 illustrates the study design for Study BEQ14993, showing two parallel crossover sequences (Sequence 1 and Sequence 2) over four periods, separated by wash-out periods.
Sequence 1: N=33 subjects. The study starts with Screening (D-21 to D-2) and Check-in (D-1), followed by Randomization. The treatment sequence is R (Reference formulation, 2 x 200mg) in Period 1, T (Test formulation, 1x 400mg) in Period 2, R in Period 3, and T in Period 4. Each period is 7 days long, followed by a Wash-out period of 8-21 days between doses. The End-of-study phase is 10-15 days after last IMP administration (4-9 days).
Sequence 2: N=33 subjects. The treatment sequence is T in Period 1, R in Period 2, T in Period 3, and R in Period 4. The wash-out periods and End-of-study phase are the same as Sequence 1.
Legend:
R: Reference formulation (2 x 200mg)
T: Test formulation (1x 400mg)
IMP: Investigational Medicinal Product

L1784: Flowchart illustrating the Study Design (4-period, 4-sequence crossover).
The study involves 12 subjects (n=12) who undergo Screening (Visit D-21 to D-2). Following screening, subjects are randomized into 4 sequences (n=3 per sequence).
Each sequence consists of four treatment periods (Period 1 to Period 4), separated by wash-out periods (≥7 days).

L1785: Figure 4.3.2.2: Mean (+SD) plasma concentrations of sotagliflozin following single dose administration of sotagliflozin as reference, p1, p2, and p3 formulations. The graph plots Mean (SD) Concentration (ng/mL) versus Time Post-dose (h). Prototype 1 shows the highest peak concentration (around 150 ng/mL) at 1 hour, followed by Prototype 2 (around 100 ng/mL) and Prototype 3 (around 50 ng/mL). All formulations show rapid clearance, with concentrations dropping below 50 ng/mL by 6 hours. The Reference formulation shows the lowest peak concentration (around 25 ng/mL) and the slowest clearance.

L1787: Figure 4.3.3.2 is a line graph showing Mean (+SD) plasma concentrations (ng/mL) versus Time Post-dose (h) for Sotagliflozin alone and following co-administration with Hydrochlorothiazide plus Sotagliflozin.
The X-axis represents Time Post-dose (h) from 0 to 24. The Y-axis represents Mean (+SD) Concentration (ng/mL) from 0 to 300.
Two curves are plotted:
Sotagliflozin (Open circles): Shows a rapid peak concentration around 2 hours, reaching approximately 150 ng/mL, followed by a gradual decline.
Hydrochlorothiazide plus Sotagliflozin (Solid line with squares): Shows a delayed peak concentration around 4 hours, reaching approximately 180 ng/mL, followed by a gradual decline.
The co-administration group generally exhibits higher peak concentrations and slightly delayed peak times compared to Sotagliflozin alone.
L1788: Figure 4.3.3.3: A line graph showing Mean (+SD) plasma concentrations of hydrochlorothiazide (HCTZ) over time (0 to 24 hours) following multiple-dose administration. The Y-axis is Mean (+SD) Concentration (ng/mL) and the X-axis is Time Post-dose (h). Two conditions are compared: HCTZ alone (circles) and HCTZ plus Sotagliflozin (squares). Both conditions show a rapid increase in concentration peaking around 2 hours, followed by a gradual decline. The concentration of HCTZ alone is consistently higher than HCTZ plus Sotagliflozin across the measured time points.

L1790: Graph showing Mean (+SD) plasma concentrations of sotagliflozin (ng/mL) versus Time Post-dose (h).

L1807: Figure 4.4.2.4 is a scatter plot showing the Simulated Median (90% Prediction Interval) Probability of Diarrhea (Y-axis, ranging from 0.0 to 1.0) versus Sotagliflozin Steady-State Cmax (ng/mL) (X-axis, ranging from 0 to 300). The median probability of diarrhea is low, starting around 0.05 at 0 ng/mL and increasing slightly to about 0.12 at 270 ng/mL. The 90% prediction intervals are shown as error bars around the median points, indicating a relatively narrow range of probability across the tested Cmax values.

L1818: Figure 4.4.3.2.13 displays four scatter plots showing the relationship between Steady-state AUC (\text{ng}\cdot\text{h}/\text{mL}) and various demographic factors (Age, Body Weight, Sex, and Race) for Phase 3 patients from a population PK dataset, using a fixed 0-order input at the population mean value (400mg QD).
AUCss vs. Age: Steady-state AUC increases significantly with age, ranging from approximately 1000 to 15000 \text{ng}\cdot\text{h}/\text{mL}.
AUCss vs. Body Weight: Steady-state AUC shows a slight negative correlation with Body Weight at Baseline (kg), ranging from approximately 0 to 15000 \text{ng}\cdot\text{h}/\text{mL}.
AUCss vs. Sex: Steady-state AUC is generally higher for Female patients (orange dots) compared to Male patients (blue triangles), with values ranging from approximately 1000 to 10000 \text{ng}\cdot\text{h}/\text{mL}.
AUCss vs. Race: Steady-state AUC is generally higher for White patients (orange dots) compared to Non-White patients (blue triangles), with values ranging from approximately 1000 to 10000 \text{ng}\cdot\text{h}/\text{mL}.

L1820: Figure 2 displays two scatter plots showing Fasting serum glucose (mg/dL) in week 23 in rats, comparing Wild-type (WT) and ZSF-1 rats treated with Sotagliflozin (Sota) or Vehicle.
The left panel shows data for WT rats. The Y-axis ranges from 0 to 500 mg/dL. The X-axis shows Sotagliflozin dose (mg/kg/day): 0 (Vehicle) and 30. WT Vehicle (green circles) shows glucose around 180 mg/dL. WT Sota 30 (red squares) shows glucose around 200 mg/dL. Statistical significance is indicated by **** (p < 0.0001) for WT Sota 30 vs WT Vehicle.
The right panel shows data for ZSF-1 rats. The Y-axis ranges from 0 to 500 mg/dL. The X-axis shows Sotagliflozin dose (mg/kg/day): 0 (Vehicle), 1, and 30. ZSF-1 Vehicle (red circles) shows glucose around 350 mg/dL. ZSF-1 Sota 1 mg/kg (orange squares) shows glucose around 250 mg/dL. ZSF-1 Sota 30 mg/kg (green triangles) shows glucose around 180 mg/dL. Statistical significance is indicated by *** (p < 0.001) for ZSF-1 Sota 1 vs ZSF-1 Vehicle, and # (p < 0.05) for ZSF-1 Sota 30 vs ZSF-1 Vehicle.
Sota = sotagliflozin.
L1821: Figure 3 displays two scatter plots showing Mean Blood Pressure (BP) in rats at 23 Weeks and the Change from week 16 (before treatment) to week 23.
The left panel, titled "23 Weeks", shows Mean BP (mmHg) on the Y-axis (80 to 180). The X-axis compares WT + vehicle, WT + Sota 30, ZSF-1 + vehicle, and ZSF-1 + Sota 30. WT groups show BP around 120 mmHg. ZSF-1 + vehicle shows BP around 140 mmHg. ZSF-1 + Sota 30 shows BP around 120 mmHg. Statistical significance is indicated by a downward arrow (p < 0.05) for ZSF-1 + Sota 30 vs ZSF-1 + vehicle.
The right panel, titled "Change from week 16 (before treatment) to week 23", shows delta Mean BP (mmHg) on the Y-axis (-40 to 40). The X-axis compares WT + vehicle, WT + Sota 30, ZSF-1 + vehicle, and ZSF-1 + Sota 30. All groups show a decrease in mean BP (negative delta). Statistical significance is indicated by downward arrows (p < 0.05) for WT + Sota 30, ZSF-1 + vehicle, and ZSF-1 + Sota 30 compared to their respective vehicle controls.
L1822: Figure 4 displays four box plots illustrating left ventricular systolic function parameters in rats during week 23, comparing WT (Wild Type) and ZSF-1 (Zucker Salt-Sensitive) rats treated with vehicle or Sota 30 (sotagliflozin 30 mg/kg/day).
Heart Rate (beats per minute): WT rats show higher heart rates (approx. 350-370 bpm) compared to ZSF-1 rats (approx. 300-320 bpm). Sota 30 treatment significantly increases heart rate in ZSF-1 rats (*p < 0.0001 vs WT Vehicle, #p < 0.0001 vs ZSF-1 Vehicle).
Ejection Fraction (%): WT rats show higher Ejection Fraction (approx. 70-80%) compared to ZSF-1 rats (approx. 60-70%). Sota 30 treatment significantly increases Ejection Fraction in ZSF-1 rats (*p < 0.0001 vs WT Vehicle, #p < 0.0001 vs ZSF-1 Vehicle).
Stroke Volume (mL): WT rats show higher Stroke Volume (approx. 280-320 mL) compared to ZSF-1 rats (approx. 250-300 mL). Sota 30 treatment significantly increases Stroke Volume in ZSF-1 rats (*p < 0.0001 vs WT Vehicle, #p < 0.0001 vs ZSF-1 Vehicle).
Cardiac Output (\mu/min): WT rats show higher Cardiac Output (approx. 100-120 \mu/min) compared to ZSF-1 rats (approx. 80-100 \mu/min). Sota 30 treatment significantly increases Cardiac Output in ZSF-1 rats (*p < 0.0001 vs WT Vehicle, #p < 0.0001 vs ZSF-1 Vehicle).
L1823: Figure 5 displays two box plots illustrating the effect of sotagliflozin on isovolumetric relaxation time (IVRT) and the ratio of IVRT to heart rate (IVRT/HR) in rats during week 23.
IVRT (ms): WT rats show IVRT values around 20-22 ms. ZSF-1 rats show significantly prolonged IVRT (approx. 25-28 ms). Sota 30 treatment significantly shortens IVRT in ZSF-1 rats (*p < 0.0001 vs WT Vehicle, #p < 0.0001 vs ZSF-1 Vehicle).
IVRT/HR (ratio): WT rats show IVRT/HR ratios around 0.05-0.07. ZSF-1 rats show significantly higher ratios (approx. 0.08-0.10). Sota 30 treatment significantly reduces the IVRT/HR ratio in ZSF-1 rats (*p < 0.0001 vs WT Vehicle, #p < 0.0001 vs ZSF-1 Vehicle).
L1824: Figure 6 displays four scatter plots showing end-diastolic wall thickness and LV diameter measurements in rats, comparing WT and ZSF-1 genotypes treated with vehicle or Sota 30 (30 mg/kg/day sotagliflozin).
The plots are:
LV anterior wall thickness (diastole): Shows measurements in mm. WT groups (green circles) show low thickness (~2.5 mm). ZSF-1 groups (red squares) show significantly higher thickness (~3.5 mm). Both ZSF-1 groups show a significant increase compared to their respective WT groups (*p < 0.0001).
LV diameter (diastole): Shows measurements in mm. WT groups show diameter around 7.5 mm. ZSF-1 groups show diameter around 8.5 mm. Both ZSF-1 groups show a significant increase compared to their respective WT groups (*p < 0.0001).
LV posterior wall thickness (diastole): Shows measurements in mm. WT groups show low thickness (~2.5 mm). ZSF-1 groups show significantly higher thickness (~3.5 mm). Both ZSF-1 groups show a significant increase compared to their respective WT groups (*p < 0.0001).
LV diameter (end-systole): Shows measurements in mm. WT groups show diameter around 4.5 mm. ZSF-1 groups show diameter around 5.5 mm. Both ZSF-1 groups show a significant increase compared to their respective WT groups (*p < 0.0001).
Legend: WKY (WT) = green circles; ZSF-1 = red squares. Sota 30 = 30 mg/kg/day sotagliflozin; WT = wild type. *p value vs. WT Vehicle, # p value vs. ZSF-1 Vehicle.
L1825: Figure 7 displays two panels showing left atrial (LA) size measurements in rats during week 23.
The left panel shows echocardiographic images of the left atrium (LA) in WT and Ob rats, with a circle indicating the measurement area.

L1826: Figure 8 displays three panels illustrating the effect of sotagliflozin (SOTA 30 mg/kg) on invasive hemodynamic parameters in wild-type (WT) and ZSF-1 rats.
Top Panel: LV Pressure (mmHg) vs Time (ms)
This panel shows LV pressure curves over time (0 to 150 ms). The ZSF obese vehicle group shows the highest peak pressure (around 190 mmHg). The ZSF obese SOTA 30 mg group shows a significantly lower peak pressure (around 170 mmHg). The WT WKY group shows the lowest peak pressure (around 150 mmHg). The legend indicates: ZSF obese vehicle (blue), ZSF obese SOTA 30 mg (red), WT WKY (yellow).
Middle Panel: LV relaxation constant TAU (ms)
This panel shows the LV relaxation constant TAU (ms) for four groups: WT + vehicle, WT + Sota 30, ZSF-1 + vehicle, and ZSF-1 + Sota 30. The ZSF-1 + vehicle group has a significantly slower relaxation constant (TAU) compared to the WT + vehicle group (p = 0.01). Chronic treatment with Sotagliflozin significantly normalizes the TAU in ZSF-1 animals (p < 0.0001 vs ZSF-1 + vehicle). There is no significant difference between WT groups (p = ns).
Bottom Panel: LVEDP (mmHg)
This panel shows Left Ventricular End-Diastolic Pressure (LVEDP) for four groups: WT + vehicle, WT + Sota 30, ZSF-1 + vehicle, and ZSF-1 + Sota 30. The ZSF-1 + vehicle group has a significantly higher LVEDP compared to the WT + vehicle group (p < 0.0001). Chronic treatment with Sotagliflozin significantly attenuates the elevated LVEDP in ZSF-1 animals (p = 0.006 vs ZSF-1 + vehicle). There is no significant difference between WT groups (p = ns).
Legend:
WT WKY (Open circles)
ZSF obese (Solid circles)
Definitions:
Sota 30 = 30 mg/kg sotagliflozin
WKY or WT = wild-type
